## Programming

Python (foundations, informatics, the language for engineers), SQL and TypeScript: notes, offline exercises and solutions, plus timed drills and coding rounds.

**Course outcomes**

By the end of the course, you can:

1. Write readable, tested Python programs that work with files, structured data, databases, packages, types, and concurrency.
2. Query, model, and change relational data safely; inspect a query plan and use SQL from Python.
3. Build strict, maintainable TypeScript programs with runtime validation, asynchronous control flow, and typed AI boundaries.
4. Read, test, and improve the Python and TypeScript examples used by the data and AI-engineering courses.
5. Say what a piece of code costs in time and memory, and where a request's time goes across the process, the operating system and the network.
6. Refactor code under tests, choose a testing strategy, debug by hypothesis, and work with others through Git, review and design docs.
7. Take a backend design from requirements to failure modes, with the contract, identity, caches, queues, limits, capacity and deployment worked out in numbers.
8. Build an accessible interface, including a streaming AI interface that shows citations, uncertainty, failure and handoff honestly.

**Before you begin**

There are no programming prerequisites. You need a terminal, Python 3.12+, the `sqlite3` CLI, and Node 22.18+ for the parts you choose to take. Start with Python 01. Begin SQL after Python 01; begin TypeScript after Python 03, when types and asynchronous work have context. The core tracks all start after Python 03. Computer science foundations and software design need nothing else. Backend and system design also needs SQL 07 and 08 and the transactions note in data engineering 01. Frontend and UX needs TypeScript.

**Completion standard and further practice**

Complete the Python track before moving to data or AI work. Take SQL when you will query or model data; take TypeScript when you will build typed applications. A complete language foundation includes a passing Python exercise set and package, checked SQL exercises and capstone, and passing TypeScript checks plus a validated asynchronous boundary. Each core track is complete when its lab passes its own checks and you can give the explanation its Evidence line asks for.

For timed or interview-format practice, use the drills. Each track's syllabus names the drills that go with a step, so do them after the lecture and notes they belong to.

### Computer science foundations

`programming/computer-science`

**What it is**

The university-level ideas behind the Python you already write, and how to reason about making it faster.

**From the syllabus**

**Purpose:** The university-level ideas behind the Python you write, and how to reason about making it faster. Math is taught where it's used. Like software design, its notes follow the full chapter standard.

**Learning objectives.** After this track, you can:

1. Count work with sums and recurrences, state a Big-O claim properly, and budget a request.
2. Explain how CPython's `list` and `dict` work, and choose a data structure by what its operations cost.
3. Recognise the common algorithm patterns and argue that they are correct.
4. Predict float error and explain why data layout changes speed by orders of magnitude.
5. Price a process, a thread and a system call, prevent races and deadlocks, and count the round trips in a cold request.

**Evidence:** Build the lab's array, hash table, LRU cache, compensated sum, frame decoder and retransmission timer against their tests, and explain a slow request by counting its round trips and its costs.

**Next:** Software design or backend and system design, in either order. The syllabus and sources are in the track README.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

Read this track after Python 03: the rows below are what it leans on, and Python 03 links back here only as further reading.

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| Python 03: Concurrency | The `asyncio` and thread pool APIs, whose underlying model chapter 5 explains |
| Python 03: Objects, inheritance and the MRO | How attribute lookup works, which chapter 2's `dict` sits under |
| Python 03: Memory and the runtime | Refcounting and free-threading, which chapters 4 and 5 build on |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| Debugging and profiling | Using a profiler to find the costs chapter 1 teaches you to predict |
| Backend and system design | Chapters 5 and 6 are its soft prerequisites: queues, the event loop and what a request costs |
| Row and column stores | Chapter 4's layout results at the scale of a database |
| Persistence | What happens after chapter 5's `flush`, including `fsync` |
| When distributed systems fail | Chapter 6's timeouts and retries across many machines |
| The hardware | Chapter 4's memory hierarchy applied to inference |

### Software design

`programming/software-design`

**What it is**

How to keep code cheap to change: reading it, refactoring it, testing it, debugging it, and working on it with other people.

**From the syllabus**

**Purpose:** Keep code cheap to change: read it, refactor it, test it, debug it, and work on it with other people. Unlike the language tracks, its notes follow the full chapter standard.

**Learning objectives.** After this track, you can:

1. Name what is wrong with a function, and pin its behaviour with characterization tests before changing its structure.
2. Apply information hiding and deep modules, and argue both sides of SOLID, DRY and clean code.
3. Choose test sizes and doubles, and tell coverage from mutation score.
4. Debug by hypothesis, and read a profile and a flame graph.
5. Reason from Git's object model, review a change for design, and write a design doc or ADR when it is worth it.

**Evidence:** Take the lab's module through refactoring under tests, then add a feature, and say honestly whether the refactoring made the feature cheaper.

**Next:** Backend and system design, and apply it in any later course. The syllabus and sources are in the track README.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| Python 03: Objects, inheritance and the MRO | Inheritance, composition and `__getattr__`, which chapters 3 and 4 weigh up |
| Python 03: Generators and iteration | The pipeline pattern in chapter 4 |
| Python 03: Memory and the runtime | Allocation and `tracemalloc`, used in chapter 6 |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| Backend and system design | Its lab is tested the way chapter 5 describes, and its debugging section starts from chapter 6 |
| Frontend: the lab | Its planted faults are chapter 5's mutation testing done by hand |
| Evals in CI | Evals take over where chapter 5's deterministic tests stop |
| Tracing and observability | Traces are the production evidence chapter 6's method starts from |
| Durable execution and interrupts | Keeping state explicit, from chapters 3 and 4, on a real agent runtime |

### Backend and system design

`programming/backend-and-system-design`

**What it is**

How a service holds up once it has real users.

**From the syllabus**

**Purpose:** How a service holds up with real users: API contracts, identity, caches and queues, balancing and limits, capacity sums, deployment and behaviour under load, then five design walkthroughs and a reference architecture for an AI system. Like software design, its notes follow the full chapter standard.

**Learning objectives.** After this track, you can:

1. Say what each API method promises, make a write survive a retry, and check identity and access per object.
2. Stop a cache stampede, handle at-least-once delivery, and choose a balancing policy and a rate limiter.
3. Write a capacity estimate as a program, deploy a container that drains correctly, and stop a retry storm.
4. Run a design from requirements to failure modes, and place each part of an AI system.

**Evidence:** Build the lab's modules against their tests, size a design from computed numbers, and say for every box on your diagram what happens to a request if it is slow, down, or running twice.

**Next:** Frontend and UX for the part a person sees; storage and compute foundations for what's underneath a store. The syllabus and sources are in the track README.

**Builds on and feeds into**

| SQL 07: modeling and DDL and SQL 08: indexes and query plans | Chapters 1 and 3 run SQLite: unique constraints, keyset pagination over a covering index, and the outbox table |
| DE 01: transactions and isolation | "Same transaction" carries the idempotency check in chapter 1 and the outbox in chapter 3 |

**Builds on** (soft prerequisites: skim when a chapter points at them)

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| CS 05: processes, threads and I/O | Little's law, backpressure and the event loop are used throughout |
| CS 06: networks | What a request costs, and what HTTP sits on |
| DE 01: partitioning and replication | Chapter 9 uses replication without re-teaching it |
| DE 01: consistency and consensus | What quorums and leases do and don't give you |
| DE 01: persistence and choosing a store | Why a write is durable, and which store fits |
| DE 01: row and column stores | The LSM tree in chapter 9 is the write-optimised side of this |
| DE 04: Kafka and stream semantics | Delivery guarantees, which chapter 3's at-least-once section applies to a queue |
| DE 05: schema and data migrations | Expand, migrate, contract, as chapter 6 uses it for a deploy |
| DE 05: operating data stores | Running what these designs depend on |
| Software design 05: testing strategy and 06: debugging and profiling | Tests for the lab, and the profiling habits behind chapter 7's debugging section |

### Frontend and UX

`programming/frontend-and-ux`

**What it is**

Enough of the browser, React, accessibility and usability for a backend, data or AI engineer to build a usable interface or judge one.

**From the syllabus**

**Purpose:** Enough of the browser, React, accessibility and usability for a backend, data or AI engineer to build a usable interface or judge one, then what AI interfaces need that ordinary ones don't: streaming, citations, uncertainty, handoff and failure. It's awareness depth, and its notes follow the full chapter standard.

**Learning objectives.** After this track, you can:

1. Pick the element that brings its behaviour for free, and model a screen as a function of state.
2. Do a ten-minute manual accessibility pass, and say what automated tools cannot see.
3. Review a screen with the usability heuristics and the three response-time limits.
4. Parse a stream and classify how it failed, check citations before showing them, show uncertainty honestly, and treat model output as untrusted.

**Evidence:** Build the lab's stream parser, state machine and chat interface against their tests, then walk every scenario by keyboard and try it with a screen reader, which no script here can do.

**Next:** Application patterns. The syllabus and sources are in the track README.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| TypeScript: JS for Python developers | The language every example here is written in |
| TypeScript: unions and narrowing | Chapter 2's state model is a discriminated union |
| TypeScript: async | Promises, `fetch` and `AbortController`, used here without being retaught |
| TypeScript: modules and tooling | How the lab's bundle and `tsc` fit together |
| TypeScript: runtime validation | The types stop at the network, which is where a stream's events come from |
| TypeScript for AI apps | The server side of streaming, and this track is the part the user sees |
| Computer science: networks | Why chunks don't respect message boundaries |
| Software design: testing strategy | The lab's planted faults are mutation testing by hand |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| Application patterns | Chat, collaborative editing and ambient agents, and what an interface does to judgment |
| Scoping an AI product | Whether to build the interface at all, and for whom |
| Real-time multimodal agents | Where the latency limits get tighter |
| Agentic search, persistence and streaming | What framework streams produce, and what this track's client has to consume |
| Generation for RAG | The plumbing behind citations |
| Backend: reliability under load | The server side of the timeouts, retries and refusals this client has to show |
| Calibration against human labels | What it takes before a confidence number deserves to be shown |
| Support agent observability | Measuring whether handoffs and fallbacks do what you hoped |
| Autonomy ladder | The human side of escalation |
| Durable execution and interrupts | An agent pausing for approval, which is a handoff going the other way |
| Threat model and prompt injection | Why model output is untrusted before it reaches the page |

### Python

`programming/python`

**What it is**

Go from your first if to async, typed, tested code.

**From the syllabus**

**Purpose:** Move from first programs to the idioms used in reliable Python services and data tooling. The track ends at the language boundary; pandas begins in Data Analytics 01.

**Learning objectives.** After this track, you can:

1. Write functions that return useful values, choose appropriate built-in collections, and handle ordinary control flow and files (Severance).
2. Extract and validate information from text, HTTP responses, JSON/XML, and a SQLite database.
3. Reason about names, objects, iteration, errors, resource lifetimes, and common Python traps (Python documentation).
4. Use type hints, dataclasses, tests, linting, packaging, and boundary validation in a small package.
5. Choose threads, asyncio, or processes for a workload and apply timeouts and concurrency limits.
6. Predict attribute lookup and the MRO, and reason about refcounting, the garbage collector and what lives in memory at runtime.

| Unit | Focus | Practice |
|---|---|---|
| 1 Foundations | Control flow, functions, strings, files and collections | Exercises |
| 2 Informatics | Text, the web, structured data, objects and databases | Exercises |
| 3 Idioms | The data model, iteration, closures and decorators, errors and context managers | Exercises |
| 4 Production habits | Types and dataclasses, packaging and tooling, concurrency | Exercises · advanced Python drills |
| 5 The runtime | Objects and the MRO, memory and the runtime | Exercise · advanced Python drills |

**Review and practice:** foundations deck · informatics deck · engineering deck · module exercises and worked solutions.

**Mastery lab:** Create a small typed Python package that reads structured input, validates it at the boundary, uses a resource safely, returns useful errors, and has tests for its core behavior. Add bounded concurrent work only where it improves the design.

**Evidence:** Your own solutions pass `python/check.sh`; the lab includes a `pyproject.toml`, typed public functions, tests, and a short explanation of its error and concurrency choices.

**Key sources:** Severance, *Python for Everybody* · VanderPlas, *A Whirlwind Tour of Python* · Python documentation · uv, Ruff, pytest, and Pydantic.

**Next:** Data Analytics 01 for pandas and tabular work; SQL for relational querying; TypeScript after the engineering unit.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| None in other subjects | The entry point of the course |
| SQL 04 Joins and SQL 07 upsert | Needed for 02 · 05 databases only |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| DA 01 Python for data analysis | Pandas starts where this track ends |
| SQL 09 SQL from Python | Parameters, DuckDB and pandas, from the Python side of databases |
| TypeScript | Starts after step 5; its async note leans on concurrency |
| Computer science foundations | Read after step 5: cost and memory arguments made on Python's own objects |
| Software design | Refactoring and patterns written in Python |

**Practice:** 98 flashcards

### SQL

`programming/sql`

**What it is**

Ask a relational database a question and know the answer is right, from a first SELECT to reading a query plan.

**From the syllabus**

**Purpose:** Learn to ask, verify, and change questions of relational data safely, from a first `SELECT` to query plans and a real investigation.

**Learning objectives.** After this track, you can:

1. Write clear `SELECT`, filtering, ordering, expression, aggregation, and conditional-aggregation queries (Software Carpentry).
2. Reason correctly about `NULL`, dates, strings, and three-valued logic.
3. Choose joins, subqueries, CTEs, recursion, and window functions without changing a result's grain accidentally.
4. Design tables with constraints, perform safe changes in transactions, and use parameterised SQL from Python.
5. Read a query plan, write sargable predicates, and select indexes appropriate to a query (Winand).

| Unit | Focus | Practice |
|---|---|---|
| 1 Query | Selection, nulls and expressions, aggregation | Exercises |
| 2 Combine | Joins, subqueries and CTEs, window functions | Exercises · window-functions drill |
| 3 Design and tune | Tables, constraints and safe changes, indexes and query plans | Exercises · index quiz |
| 4 Use | SQL from Python, and the SQL Murder Mystery capstone | Exercise · setup and data |

**Review and practice:** SQL deck · offline SQL/Python exercises and solutions · the capstone investigation.

**Mastery lab:** Solve the capstone with a query trail that another person can reproduce. Add one analytical query using a window function and one safe data-change or parameterised Python example, explaining grain, `NULL` behavior, and validation checks.

**Evidence:** Your queries pass `sql/practice/check.sh`. The capstone includes the answer, the queries that establish it, and a short explanation of why each join cannot fan out the result.

**Key sources:** Software Carpentry: Databases and SQL · PGExercises · SQL Murder Mystery · Use The Index, Luke · SQLite documentation.

**Next:** Data Analytics for analytical interpretation and pandas; Data Engineering for data systems and pipelines.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| Python 01 Foundations | SQL starts after it, and 09 drives SQL from Python |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| DA 06/06 SQL for analytics | Analytical SQL on top of these joins and window functions |
| Python 02/05 Databases | Hands the SQL itself to this track |
| Backend and system design | Its chapters 1 and 3 run SQLite and assume 07 and 08 from the first page |

**Practice:** 1 deck · 29 flashcards

- **[2 — Missing Data, Dates and Strings](../data/courses/programming/sql/notes/02-nulls-and-expressions.md)** — The same applies to NOT IN. If the subquery returns even one NULL, x NOT IN (…) is never true, so the query returns nothing.
  - Wiki: [SQL NULL and Three-Valued Logic](../data/wiki/programming/sql-null-three-valued-logic.md)
  - Nearby (embedding only): [SQL Query Fundamentals](../data/wiki/programming/sql-query-fundamentals.md)
- **[3 — Aggregation](../data/courses/programming/sql/notes/03-aggregation.md)** — WHERE runs before grouping, so it can't see aggregates.
  - Wiki: [SQL Query Fundamentals](../data/wiki/programming/sql-query-fundamentals.md)
- **[4 — Joins](../data/courses/programming/sql/notes/04-joins.md)** — Conceptually, the database builds every pair of rows (the cross product) and keeps the pairs where the ON condition is true.
  - Wiki: [SQL Join Patterns](../data/wiki/programming/sql-join-patterns.md)

- **[6 — Window Functions](../data/courses/programming/sql/notes/06-window-functions.md)** — Window functions are evaluated after WHERE, GROUP BY and HAVING, so you can't filter on them in the same query.
  - Wiki: [SQL Window Functions](../data/wiki/programming/sql-window-functions.md)
- **[7 — Designing Tables and Changing Data](../data/courses/programming/sql/notes/07-modeling-and-ddl.md)** — Breaking rule 3 on purpose, by denormalising, is a legitimate choice for analytics tables built by a pipeline.

### TypeScript

`programming/typescript`

**What it is**

Go from "JavaScript for Python developers" to typed LLM applications.

**From the syllabus**

**Purpose:** Move from JavaScript fluency to a strict type system, safe runtime boundaries, and the async patterns required in typed applications and AI integrations.

**Learning objectives.** After this track, you can:

1. Translate Python habits into idiomatic JavaScript while avoiding coercion, sorting, and truthiness traps.
2. Model application data with interfaces, types, unions, narrowing, generics, and type transformations (TypeScript Handbook).
3. Configure a strict TypeScript project, describe modules, and make type-level guarantees executable through tests.
4. Write async work with timeouts, retries, parallelism, and concurrency limits.
5. Validate environment variables, JSON, and model output at runtime rather than trusting erased types (Zod).
6. Build a typed tool-loop or streaming AI integration that narrows content blocks and handles stop reasons safely.

| Unit | Focus | Practice |
|---|---|---|
| 1 JavaScript and core types | JavaScript for Python developers, everyday types, unions and narrowing | Type exercises |
| 2 Type-level design | Generics and transformations from types | Exercises and type challenges · typed `groupBy` drill |
| 3 Production boundaries | Async, modules and tooling, runtime validation, TypeScript for AI apps | Runtime exercises · ESLint drill |

**Review and practice:** TypeScript deck · vendored type exercises and challenges · runtime exercises with tests. The live SDK example is optional and requires its own key; it is not required for mastery.

**Mastery lab:** Build a small strict TypeScript boundary: parse untrusted input with Zod, model outcomes as a discriminated union, run independent work concurrently with a limit and timeout, and test success and failure paths. An optional extension can call a model or tool API.

**Evidence:** The implementation and its tests pass `npm run check`; explain the runtime boundary and how the union makes an unhandled outcome impossible to ignore.

**Key sources:** TypeScript Handbook · typescript-exercises · type-challenges · Zod · Anthropic TypeScript SDK.

**Next:** Frontend and UX to put a typed program in front of a person; AI Engineering for production LLM systems, or Generative AI for LLM foundations and applications.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| Python 03 Language for engineers | TypeScript starts after it; types and asynchronous work have context by then |
| Python 03/07 Concurrency | The asyncio model that async compares with promises |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| Frontend and UX | Starts after this track; React and AI interfaces use its async and streaming |
| DA 05/06 Dashboard stacks | The TypeScript end of a dashboard stack |
| AI Engineering | Labs come in TypeScript and Python side by side |

## Data Analytics

Processing data, probability and statistics, visualisation and storytelling, experimentation and causality, applied analyses, Bayesian analysis.

**Course outcomes**

By the end of the course, you can:

1. Create a reproducible analysis-ready dataset and verify that transformations preserved its meaning.
2. Describe variation and uncertainty, select an appropriate statistical method, and qualify a quantitative claim.
3. Design and validate an experiment; distinguish association from credible causal evidence.
4. Build and diagnose Bayesian models, then make decisions with explicit uncertainty and costs.
5. Create clear, truthful visualisations and use them to make a recommendation.
6. Complete an end-to-end analysis with a baseline, validation, operational implication, and limitations.

**Before you begin**

**Required:** Python fundamentals through modules 01 to 03 of Programming: Python. You should be comfortable with variables, functions, collections, files, and a terminal. No pandas or statistics course is assumed. **Helpful alongside this course:** Programming: SQL. Unit 06 applies analytical SQL; the SQL track teaches the language in depth.

**Course language:** Python. The main tools are pandas, NumPy, SciPy, statsmodels, PyMC, matplotlib, seaborn, Plotly, and DuckDB. Each pillar README covers its own setup and how to run its practice.

**Completion standard and next steps**

You have completed the course when your portfolio demonstrates the six unit outcomes: a reproducible data-preparation analysis, a statistical memo, an experiment or causal-design brief, a diagnosed Bayesian decision analysis, a visual recommendation, and two end-to-end applied cases. Correct code alone is not enough: every piece should name its decision, unit of analysis, checks, uncertainty, and limitations.

Continue to Data Science for predictive modelling and model research; Data Engineering for pipelines and infrastructure; or Generative AI for LLM-powered applications.

### Processing data

`data-analytics/01-python-for-data-analysis`

**What it is**

Turn a raw export into a table you can trust.

**From the syllabus**

**Purpose:** Turn a raw export into a table you can safely analyse. Cleaning, joins, aggregation, time, and charts are correctness problems, not merely API calls (McKinney; Wickham on tidy shape).

**Learning objectives.** After this unit, you can:

1. Inspect a DataFrame's shape, types, labels, and missingness before using it.
2. Load a messy export into a rerunnable cleaning workflow and account for every changed or removed row.
3. Select, filter, and update data safely using label- and position-based indexing.
4. Reshape and join tables while specifying join semantics, validating cardinality, and reconciling totals.
5. Compute grouped summaries and rates correctly, including when groups or denominators are missing.
6. Treat dates as a type, and summarise over time with resampling and windows that look backward.

**Review and practice:** deck · chapter `Try it` exercises · progressive practice notebooks.

**Mastery lab:** From a raw retail-style export, create a documented analysis-ready table. Show a rerunnable cleaning workflow, validated join, correctly aggregated metric, and one chart with a plain-language interpretation.

**Evidence:** Preserve the cleaning function, before/after row counts, and a reconciled business total. State `how` and `validate` for the join; show the numerator and denominator for a rate.

**Key sources:** Wes McKinney, *Python for Data Analysis* · Jake VanderPlas, *Python Data Science Handbook* · Wickham, *Tidy Data* · Kaggle Learn: Pandas.

| SQL track | Helpful, not required: selecting, grouping and joining return here on a table in memory |

### Probability and statistics

`data-analytics/02-probability-and-statistics`

**What it is**

Say how far a claim about data you have not seen can be trusted.

**From the syllabus**

**Purpose:** Make claims about data you have not seen without overstating what a sample says. This is the vocabulary for later experiments, models, and evaluations.

**Learning objectives.** After this unit, you can:

1. Audit an extract's unit of observation and distribution, then choose summaries that represent it honestly.
2. Use conditional probability, expectation, and variance to reason about uncertainty and base rates.
3. Match common analytical measures to probability distributions and identify when sampling assumptions fail.
4. Estimate quantities with intervals, bootstrap procedures, and sample-size reasoning.
5. Select, run, and interpret a hypothesis test, including an appropriate multiple-comparison correction (Wasserstein and Lazar on what a p-value does not say).
6. Fit and interpret linear, logistic, and Poisson regression models with useful uncertainty checks (James et al.).
7. Say what a model claims: write the data generating process you believe, and separate a better fit from better generalisation (The data generating process).
8. Compare units with themselves using panel data and fixed effects, and say what that does and does not remove (Panel data and fixed effects).

**Review and practice:** deck · chapter `Try it` exercises and interview checks · optional guided statistical-analysis notebook.

**Mastery lab:** Audit an unfamiliar extract, state the estimand and independent unit, report a point estimate and interval, answer one predeclared comparison, then fit and interpret a suitable regression with diagnostics.

**Evidence:** A statistical memo containing the unit of analysis, distribution or visual checks, interval, justified test or model, effect size, and limits of the conclusion.

**Key sources:** Bruce & Bruce, *Practical Statistics for Data Scientists* · Downey, *Think Stats* · James et al., *An Introduction to Statistical Learning with Applications in Python* · MIT 18.650 · Wasserstein and Lazar, ASA Statement on p-Values.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| None in other subjects | Starts from DA 01 in this subject |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| DS 01/01 Statistical foundations | Takes conditional probability, expectation and maximum likelihood as given |
| DS 01/04 Model evaluation | The same two error cells, and the bootstrap for a metric's interval |
| GA 08/09 Reading results | Standard errors, paired tests and multiple comparisons on pass rates |
| GA 08/15 Calibrating a judge | Correcting a judge's pass rate is the same accounting as a base-rate calculation |
| AIE 09/03 Guardrail models | Shows the base-rate result on a safety classifier |
| DE 05/01 Quality dimensions and tests | EDA's checks for impossible values become pipeline tests |

### Visualisation and storytelling

`data-analytics/05-visualisation-and-storytelling`

**What it is**

How to choose a chart, draw it well in Python and JavaScript, and use it to get a decision made.

**From the syllabus**

**Purpose:** Make an analytical result legible and useful. Select encodings, build static and interactive charts, and shape them into an argument without overstating what the data says.

**Learning objectives.** After this unit, you can:

1. Match a question and data type to a chart using perceptually strong encodings and honest scales (Cleveland and McGill).
2. Build clear static charts with matplotlib and seaborn, including annotations and uncertainty where needed.
3. Design interactive charts with useful filtering, tooltips, and accessible fallbacks.
4. Explain when to use Python plotting libraries, D3, Vega-Lite, or Observable Plot, and implement a small web chart.
5. Design a dashboard and narrative that give a decision-maker context, evidence, recommendation, and limitations (Knaflic).
6. Choose a dashboard stack by audience, interaction, data volume and maintainer, and get Python results to a browser without leaking or overloading (chapter 6).

**Review and practice:** deck · chapter exercises · runnable practice scripts and dashboard.

**Mastery lab:** Take one decision from an earlier unit and create a concise visual narrative or dashboard: audience and decision, chart choice, source and transformation notes, recommendation, and the limitation most likely to change it.

**Evidence:** A reviewer can understand the main claim without the code, trace it to a valid measure, and identify the action, uncertainty, and guardrail. Include chart source and an accessible static fallback for interactive work.

**Key sources:** Knaflic, *Storytelling with Data* · Wilke, *Fundamentals of Data Visualization* · Munzner, *Visualization Analysis and Design* · Cleveland and McGill, *Graphical Perception* · Kaggle Learn: Data Visualization.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| DA 01 Processing data | The table every chart here starts from; chapter 1b plots its orders and its year of daily revenue |
| Python 02/03 Web data | HTTP and JSON, for how results reach a browser in chapter 6 |
| TypeScript 01 JavaScript for Python developers | Optional, for reading the React front end in chapter 6 |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| GA 06/06 Cost and latency dashboards | What a dashboard is for, applied to LLM traces |
| GA 07/05 Data-analysis agent | An agent's charts answer to the same standards |

### Experimentation and causality

`data-analytics/03-experimentation-and-causality`

**What it is**

Answer whether a change caused a result, with an experiment when you can run one and with a design you can defend when you cannot.

**From the syllabus**

**Purpose:** Answer whether a change caused an outcome. Learn the discipline that makes an A/B result trustworthy and the assumptions required when randomisation is unavailable.

**Learning objectives.** After this unit, you can:

1. Design an A/B test: randomisation unit, estimand, MDE, sample size, stopping rule, and reporting plan (Kohavi et al. 2009).
2. Select a north-star metric, guardrail, and diagnostic metric; interpret ratios and denominator changes correctly.
3. Validate an experiment before trusting its outcome, including SRM, balance, and unit-of-analysis checks (Dmitriev et al.).
4. Apply CUPED and suitable sequential testing, and explain interference and overlapping-experiment risks.
5. Draw a causal graph and distinguish confounders, mediators, and colliders.
6. Choose and qualify DiD, IV, RD, weighting, or synthetic control, naming its identifying assumption (Cunningham).
7. Build a comparison group by matching or weighting, show balance before reading the outcome, and say what a hidden confounder would have to do to undo the result (Matching and weighting).

**Review and practice:** deck · runnable chapter examples and drills, including the Larkspur checkout experiment and causal-reasoning examples.

**Mastery lab:** Analyse a checkout experiment and write a launch/no-launch brief. For an observational question, instead submit a causal diagram, proposed design, and the assumption that makes the estimate interpretable.

**Evidence:** State the decision, OEC and guardrails, sample-size and stopping logic; validate assignment; report effect and uncertainty; and explain the recommendation or why evidence is insufficient.

**Key sources:** Kohavi, Tang, and Xu, *Trustworthy Online Controlled Experiments* · Kohavi et al., *Controlled Experiments on the Web* · Dmitriev et al., *A Dirty Dozen* · Cunningham, *Causal Inference: The Mixtape* · Brady Neal's course.

| AIE 09/02 Prompt injection and jailbreaks | Utility without attack works as a guardrail metric |
| DS 05/03 Two-tower and evaluation | Online evaluation of a recommender is an A/B test |

### Applied analyses

`data-analytics/06-applied-analyses`

**What it is**

Complete analyses, each going from a business question through a dataset to a recommendation someone could act on.

**From the syllabus**

**Purpose:** Do the complete job. Each case begins with a decision and ends with a recommendation, using earlier units to establish a baseline, validate results, and make value concrete.

**Learning objectives.** After this unit, you can:

1. Frame an analytical request as a decision, metric, baseline, and validation plan.
2. Forecast demand with multiple baselines, rolling-origin backtests, and an operational recommendation (Hyndman and Athanasopoulos).
3. Build and evaluate a credit-scorecard-style analysis while distinguishing predictive performance, calibration, drift, and policy constraints.
4. Diagnose retention and conversion with cohorts and funnels, separating rate from mix and association from causation (Fader and Hardie).
5. Segment customers responsibly, validate later usefulness, and distinguish targeting from persuadability.
6. Use analytical SQL to reconcile changes in a business total and explain their drivers.
7. Model time to an event, treating customers who have not left yet as censored (Survival and event history).
8. Model customers as states and sequences, and check a transition matrix on a holdout before using it to forecast (States and sequences).
9. Separate attribution from incrementality when you allocate a marketing budget (Marketing measurement).
10. Turn a forecast's error distribution into stock and staffing decisions (Operations and supply chain).
11. Design a survey, weight it to known totals, and report what its margin of error omits (Surveys and sampling design).

**Review and practice:** deck · runnable case-study scripts and their generated or documented datasets.

**Mastery portfolio:** Complete at least two cases, including forecasting or credit scoring and one of cohort, funnel, segmentation, or SQL. For each: frame the decision, show a baseline, validate against something known, quantify an operational or financial implication, recommend an action, and state what could overturn it.

**Evidence:** A reproducible analysis, decision memo, and clear visual. Reconcile reported totals to the source, distinguish prediction from a causal claim, and place uncertainty beside the recommendation.

**Key sources:** Hyndman and Athanasopoulos, *Forecasting: Principles and Practice* · Grömping, *South German Credit Data* · Fader and Hardie, *How to Project Customer Retention* · Fader, Hardie, and Lee, *RFM and CLV* · Mode SQL tutorial.

### Bayesian analysis

`data-analytics/04-bayesian-analysis`

**What it is**

Treat unknowns as distributions, update them with data, and choose the action with the lowest expected cost.

**From the syllabus**

**Purpose:** Revisit inference as a decision problem. Use prior information, posterior uncertainty, diagnostics, and losses to answer what should be done, not only whether a null hypothesis is surprising (Davidson-Pilon).

**Learning objectives.** After this unit, you can:

1. Express an analytical question as a generative model and compute or simulate a posterior.
2. Fit a basic Bayesian model in PyMC and interpret posterior probabilities and credible intervals.
3. Diagnose sampling quality with R-hat, ESS, traces, and divergences, then improve an ill-behaved model.
4. Select, justify, and pressure-test priors with prior-predictive simulation.
5. Compare variants in a Bayesian A/B test using probability of superiority, lift uncertainty, and expected loss.
6. Translate posterior uncertainty into a decision by defining an appropriate loss function.
7. Fit a multilevel model, choose between clustering and modelling a group, and read many segment effects with partial pooling (Multilevel models and partial pooling).

**Review and practice:** deck · fixed-seed chapter examples · optional advanced Bayesian ML coursework.

**Mastery lab:** Reanalyse the checkout A/B case with a PyMC model. Run prior-predictive and sampler checks, quantify posterior uncertainty, define the cost of each mistake, and make a ship/no-ship recommendation.

**Evidence:** Submit a reproducible model, diagnostic summary, posterior answer, loss-based recommendation, and a brief comparison with the fixed-horizon frequentist analysis. The advanced coursework is optional archival material, not required practice.

**Key sources:** Davidson-Pilon, *Bayesian Methods for Hackers* · McElreath's lectures · Gelman et al., *Bayesian Data Analysis* · Aalto Bayesian Data Analysis · PyMC examples.

## Data Science

Classical ML, deep learning, NLP, time series and recommenders.

**Course outcomes**

By the end of this course, you will be able to:

1. Frame a prediction problem, construct a valid split, and establish a baseline that later models must beat.
2. Train, tune, evaluate, and explain classical models for tabular data without leakage or misleading metrics.
3. Build and debug neural networks, selecting architectures and interventions from measured failure modes.
4. Represent text as features, compare sparse and learned representations, and evaluate NLP systems honestly.
5. Forecast time-dependent data and build recommender systems using evaluation that respects time, ranking, and logged-policy bias.
6. Communicate model performance, uncertainty, error costs, and operational limitations to a decision-maker.

**Before you begin**

**Required:** Data Analytics, especially probability, estimation, hypothesis testing, regression, experimentation, and the practice of working safely with data frames. You also need Python and NumPy fluency from Programming: Python.

**Needed for some units:** calculus through the chain rule and matrix products for neural-network fundamentals. Bayesian modelling remains in Data Analytics and is not repeated here.

**Course language:** Python. Primary libraries include scikit-learn, PyTorch, pandas, NumPy, matplotlib, and selected NLP and recommender packages. Each pillar README covers its own setup and datasets.

**Completion standard and next steps**

You have completed this course when your portfolio includes: a documented tabular-model comparison, a neural-training diagnosis, a text-model comparison, and either a time-series or recommender evaluation. Each artifact should identify its unit, split, baseline, metric, errors, limitations, and deployment or experiment decision.

Continue to Generative AI for LLMs, retrieval, agents, and evaluation; to Data Engineering for production data and ML infrastructure; or to AI Engineering for reliable LLM systems.

**Previous subject:** data analytics · **Next subject:** generative AI

### Classical ML

`data-science/01-classical-ml`

**What it is**

The classical machine-learning toolkit for tabular data: linear models, trees, kernels, clustering and boosting, with the practices that make their scores believable. This is where a data scientist moves beyond an analyst: an analyst describes last year, and a data scientist predicts the next one and can say whether the prediction holds.

**From the syllabus**

**Purpose:** Learn the full modelling loop for tabular data: formulate the target, avoid leakage, select a metric from error costs, compare baselines, tune responsibly, and explain the resulting model without mistaking explanation for causation.

**Learning objectives.** After this unit, you can:

1. Explain generalisation, bias, variance, and the train/validation/test contract (James et al.).
2. Select a supervised model family and control complexity for a tabular prediction problem (Grinsztajn et al. on why trees still lead there).
3. Build leakage-safe preprocessing and feature engineering pipelines.
4. Choose a metric and threshold that reflect the costs of false positives and false negatives, then compare against a dummy baseline.
5. Use clustering or dimensionality reduction cautiously, on numeric, categorical or mixed columns, validate the result, and avoid treating visual structure as proof.
6. Compare ensembles and boosting libraries across seeds, tune with cross-validation and early stopping, and report uncertainty in a claimed improvement.
7. Use permutation importance, PDPs, and SHAP appropriately while distinguishing model explanations from causal claims.
8. Diagnose imbalance, recurring errors, and distribution shift before deploying a model.
9. Start from the target to pick the family, loss, metric and baseline (Choosing a metric).
10. Read false positives and false negatives before tuning, and fix a class of error on one split while scoring on another (Reading the errors).
11. Split predictions into automate, review and reject, pricing the thresholds from error costs and review time (Automate, review or reject).
12. Learn from corrections without trusting silence: measure label noise, test active learning against random, and audit a random sample (Learning from corrections).
13. Measure group gaps in a screening model, say which of them cannot be closed together, and state what an audit must report (Fairness in automated scoring).
14. Detect anomalies with a density model, a classifier or an isolation forest, and fit a matrix-factorisation recommender on a ratings matrix (Unsupervised learning; DS 05 takes recommenders further).

**Review and practice:** deck · chapter exercises · boosting comparison · credit-default lab · music-clustering lab.

**Mastery lab:** Build a leakage-safe prediction pipeline for a tabular decision. Compare a dummy, linear model, and boosted-tree model across appropriate splits; inspect the errors; select a threshold from costs; and explain the result with a checked explanation method.

**Evidence:** A concise model card with target and unit, split rationale, baselines, metric and threshold rationale, cross-validation or seed variation, error slices, explanation, limitations, and the condition that would stop deployment.

**Key sources:** James et al., *An Introduction to Statistical Learning* · Müller and Guido, *Introduction to Machine Learning with Python* · scikit-learn User Guide · Grinsztajn et al., *Why do tree-based models still outperform deep learning on tabular data?*.

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| DA 02/02 Probability foundations | Conditional probability, expectation and variance |
| DA 02/04 Estimation | Bias, variance, maximum likelihood, the bootstrap and Wilson intervals |
| DA 02/05 Hypothesis testing | Type I and II errors and multiple comparisons |
| DA 02/06 Regression and GLMs | Inference for linear models, which scikit-learn leaves out |
| DA 03/04 Correlation and causation | Why a model's explanations are not causal |
| DA 06/02 Credit scoring | The scorecard that the imbalance chapter starts from |
| DA 02/07 The data generating process | A model is an estimate of f, and adjusted R² for comparing models, which note 11 uses |
| DA 04/07 Multilevel models | Partial pooling for small slices, which note 12 points to |

### Deep learning

`data-science/02-deep-learning`

**What it is**

Neural networks from the forward pass to pre-trained transformers, split along the five courses of Ng's Deep Learning Specialization, with a closing chapter on text models. Ng's coursework stays in `practice/` as it was submitted, in NumPy and TensorFlow 1.x. The chapters use PyTorch as the course framework, and note 01 has a table for translating any notebook to it.

**From the syllabus**

**Purpose:** Train a neural network deliberately, locate the source of failure when it does not learn, and match an architecture to the structure of image, sequence, and text data.

**Learning objectives.** After this unit, you can:

1. Derive a forward pass and backpropagation for a small network, then validate gradients and initial loss (Goodfellow et al.).
2. Diagnose optimisation and generalisation problems using activations, learning curves, learning-rate sweeps, regularisation, and clean validation data.
3. Use gap analysis to choose the intervention with the largest plausible gain.
4. Explain why convolutions, residual connections, and data augmentation suit image tasks (He et al.), and choose between training from scratch, a frozen pretrained backbone and fine-tuning from a measured comparison on your own data size (note 04b).
5. Build and diagnose sequence models, including recurrent networks, attention and transformer encoders, for ordered inputs (Vaswani et al.).
6. Compare TF-IDF, frozen encoders, and fine-tuned text models against the same held-out baseline.

**Review and practice:** deck · runnable chapter examples in PyTorch · optional historical Ng coursework for comparison.

**Mastery lab:** Train a small neural model with a linear or from-scratch control. Diagnose one deliberate failure (such as poor initialisation, train/eval mode, or learning rate), correct it, and justify the next experiment from the measurements.

**Evidence:** A training report containing the control, data split, learning curves, initialisation and optimiser choices, error analysis, final metric, and an explanation of why the chosen architecture fits the data.

**Key sources:** Goodfellow, Bengio, and Courville, *Deep Learning* · PyTorch tutorials · He et al., *Deep Residual Learning* · Vaswani et al., *Attention Is All You Need*.

### Natural language processing

`data-science/03-nlp`

**What it is**

The pre-transformer NLP toolkit, taught as something you can still use: preprocessing, bag of words and TF-IDF, text classification, topic models and summarisation, word embeddings, named entity recognition and sentiment analysis. It closes on the modern shortcut, in which sentence embeddings from a pretrained encoder become ordinary features for classification, clustering and anomaly detection.

**From the syllabus**

**Purpose:** Make text measurable. Establish the sparse baseline first, then learn when embeddings, pretrained encoders, and sequence methods earn their added complexity.

**Learning objectives.** After this unit, you can:

1. Choose and justify a text representation, including tokenisation, normalisation, bag-of-words, TF-IDF, and embeddings (Jurafsky and Martin).
2. Train and assess a text classifier against a TF-IDF linear baseline using leakage-safe evaluation.
3. Explore an unlabelled corpus with topics or summaries while validating interpretations against source documents.
4. Use word and sentence embeddings for similarity, retrieval-style ranking, classification, clustering, and anomaly detection (Mikolov et al.; Reimers and Gurevych).
5. Evaluate semantic analysis, NER, and sentiment systems with the correct unit and metric.
6. Compare a pretrained encoder with sparse features while accounting for label quantity, compute cost, and result variation.

**Review and practice:** deck · reproducible note runner · chapter drills. Older course folders remain optional reference material rather than required labs.

**Mastery lab:** Choose a labelled text problem and compare a TF-IDF linear model with a pretrained-embedding approach. Include a split-safe vectorisation pipeline, macro-F1 or another justified metric, sample error review, cost/latency trade-off, and a recommendation.

**Evidence:** A short text-model report with representation rationale, baseline and candidate comparison, variance or interval where appropriate, confusion/error examples, and a documented limitation.

**Key sources:** Jurafsky and Martin, *Speech and Language Processing* · scikit-learn text-feature guide · Mikolov et al., *Efficient Estimation of Word Representations* · Reimers and Gurevych, *Sentence-BERT*.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| DA 04/01 Bayesian inference | Conjugacy and generative stories behind LDA |
| DA 04/02 MCMC and PyMC | Samplers and variational inference |
| DA 06/05 Segmentation and RFM | Scoring clusters against an outcome |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| GA 01/01 Tokens and embeddings | Word counts to word2vec to a learned embedding table |
| GA 02/03 Keyword, semantic and hybrid search | TF-IDF, BM25 and sentence embeddings are taught here first |
| GA 09/03 nanoGPT | Compares a GPT with these earlier text models |
| AIE 05/10 Building KGs with LLMs | NER without an LLM |
| AIE 08/03 Multimodal RAG | Comparing vectors in one space |

**Practice:** 1 deck · 100 flashcards

**Notes** (10, in reading order)

- **[03 — Natural language processing](../data/courses/data-science/03-nlp/readme.md)** — The pre-transformer NLP toolkit, taught as something you can still use: preprocessing, bag of words and TF-IDF, text classification, topic models and summarisation, word embeddings, named entity recognition and sentiment analysis.
  - Wiki: none

*Notes*

- **[Text representation](../data/courses/data-science/03-nlp/notes/01-text-representation.md)** — A model cannot read. ### Time series and recommenders

`data-science/05-time-series-and-recommenders`

**What it is**

Time series ML turns a history into a forecast using feature tables, gradient boosting and sequence models, tested with backtests. Recommender systems learn preferences from interaction logs using matrix factorisation, linear item models and two-tower networks, and are tested on full-catalogue ranking, counterfactual estimates and A/B tests. Entity matching applies the same retrieve-then-rank shape to joining records with no shared key, using blocking and learning to rank, and an encoder tuned on match labels supplies its candidates.

**From the syllabus**

**Purpose:** Handle two cases where rows are not exchangeable: predicting the future and ranking a catalogue for a person. In both, evaluation design is the primary technical skill.

**Learning objectives.** After this unit, you can:

1. Define an issue time, forecast horizon, and legal information set, then evaluate forecasts with rolling backtests (Hyndman and Athanasopoulos).
2. Compare feature-based and sequence forecasting models against an appropriate naive baseline without temporal leakage.
3. Build collaborative-filtering and factorisation recommenders and evaluate ranking rather than only rating error (Koren et al.).
4. Explain how two-tower retrieval handles large catalogues and cold-start content features.
5. Design offline and online evaluation for ranking systems, including sampled-negative, exposure-bias, and counterfactual-evaluation pitfalls (Dacrema et al.).
6. Match records with no shared key by blocking, scoring and ranking candidates, train pointwise, pairwise and listwise rankers, and score a match by whole records with room to abstain (Fellegi and Sunter; Burges).
7. Decide when an encoder is worth tuning, build training pairs from match labels without leaking matches, and measure what hard-negative mining changes (Musgrave et al.; Huang et al.).

**Review and practice:** deck · labs in practice · customer-segments lab · matching lab · similarity lab.

**Mastery lab:** Complete either a rolling-origin demand forecast or a recommendation-ranking comparison. State the information boundary, include the naive or popularity baseline, show the correct evaluation metric, inspect failure cases, and convert the model choice into an operational decision.

**Evidence:** A reproducible notebook or script with temporal or interaction split, baseline comparison, metric rationale, leakage audit, result limitations, and a plan for the online test required before rollout.

**Key sources:** Hyndman and Athanasopoulos, *Forecasting: Principles and Practice* · Koren, Bell, and Volinsky, *Matrix Factorization Techniques for Recommender Systems* · Rendle et al., *BPR* · Dacrema et al., *Are We Really Making Much Progress?*.

### generative-ai/09-llms-from-scratch (taught in another course)

**Purpose:** Build the neural machinery behind a language model once, so later generative-AI abstractions remain inspectable (Karpathy). This sequence is hosted in Generative AI to keep its code and prerequisites together.

**Learning objectives.** After this bridge, you can:

1. Implement automatic differentiation and backpropagation in a minimal scalar engine.
2. Train a character-level language model and diagnose its loss, embeddings, activations, and optimisation behaviour.
3. Build and train a small GPT, explaining the relationship between attention, logits, sampling, and generation.

**Learning:** the LLMs from scratch pillar and its practice.

**Evidence:** A short implementation note that validates gradients, explains the expected initial loss, and shows a small generative model improving against a baseline.

**Key source:** Karpathy, *Neural Networks: Zero to Hero*.

## Data Engineering

Storage and compute foundations, ingest and modelling, transform and orchestrate, warehouse, lakehouse and streaming, quality and observability, ML platform, architecture and governance.

**Course outcomes**

By the end, you can build repeatable ingestion and well-modelled data; deliver tested, recoverable pipelines; choose storage and streaming systems by correctness, latency, and cost; detect and repair data incidents; build ML-platform foundations; and make defensible architecture and governance decisions.

**Before you begin**

**Required:** Programming: Python modules 01 to 03 and SQL up to its capstone. The zoomcamps assume SQL and teach none. Docker basics help, and they are introduced in early practice. Data Analytics is recommended, not required.

**Learning model:** read the unit's chapters, use its deck where present, complete drills and practice, then keep the stated evidence of mastery. Core examples run locally; cloud coursework is optional enrichment, with local alternatives described in pillar READMEs.

**Completion standard and next steps**

Complete a portfolio containing a repeatable ingest/model, tested and orchestrated pipeline, costed storage or streaming decision, quality incident response, ML-platform exercise, and governed architecture decision. Every item should state its grain or contract, failure handling, evidence, and trade-offs. The capstone is MLOps `07-project` or the DE project brief: self-score against the evaluation criteria in the brief, with LocalStack standing in for the cloud criterion.

Continue to Data Science for modelling depth, or Generative AI and AI Engineering for LLM systems.

### Storage and compute foundations

`data-engineering/01-storage-and-compute-foundations`

**What it is**

The logic that the rest of data engineering rests on: what a data system promises, how data is encoded and laid out, what happens when two writers meet, how data is split and copied across machines, what goes wrong there, how machines agree…

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** define reliability and performance goals; choose data models and encodings (Kleppmann 2017, ch. 1–4); explain row versus column storage; reproduce and prevent transaction anomalies (ch. 7); reason about replication, partitioning (ch. 5–6), clocks, consensus (ch. 8–9), and distributed compute (ch. 10); say what a commit promises under each durability setting; pick a store from its access pattern.

**Learning:** reading order, chapter code, and practice. **Evidence:** measure a representative workload, explain a failure mode, and defend the smallest system that meets the requirement.

### Ingest and modelling

`data-engineering/02-ingest-and-modelling`

**What it is**

How data enters a platform you control, and what shape it takes once it is there.

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** make loads repeatable; choose batch, CDC, or log ingestion (Kleppmann 2017, ch. 11); declare table grain; model facts, dimensions, and slowly changing attributes (Kimball and Ross); compare Data Vault and One Big Table; write a data contract (Breck et al., *Data Validation for Machine Learning*).

**Learning:** reading order and DataTalks practice. **Evidence:** a rerunnable load, star-model grain statement, reconciliation check, and contract that rejects a bad batch. **Book chapters:** Kleppmann 3, 4, 11, and 12.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| Programming | Python and SQL fluency, which the zoomcamps assume |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| None in other subjects | Nothing outside this subject links here yet |

**Practice:** 1 deck · 66 flashcards

### Transform and orchestrate

`data-engineering/03-transform-and-orchestrate`

**What it is**

How landed tables become tested models, and how those models get built on schedule, in the right order, for the right day.

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** structure transformations; test grain and joins; use dbt tests, snapshots, and incremental models (dbt documentation); reason about logical dates, retries, and backfills (Kleppmann 2017, ch. 8–10); compare Dagster and Airflow.

**Learning:** reading order, runnable chapter code, and practice. **Evidence:** a green tested model plus a backfill that produces identical output when rerun.

**Builds on and feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| Programming | Python and SQL fluency, which the zoomcamps assume |

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| DA 06/06 SQL for analytics | The kind of question an analyst asks of a mart |

### Warehouse, lakehouse and streaming

`data-engineering/04-warehouse-lakehouse-streaming`

**What it is**

Everything else: references.md.

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** estimate scan cost; select layout and partitioning; explain table-format transactions (Armbrust et al.); evaluate lakehouse trade-offs; implement safe producer/consumer semantics; reason about event time, watermarks, and lag (Akidau et al.); compare warehouses by concept (storage and compute separation, layout, concurrency); reason about object store consistency and cost.

**Learning:** reading order, deck, and labelled practice. **Evidence:** a costed store decision plus a stream design that says exactly-once from where to where. **Book chapters and docs:** Kleppmann 3, 6, 7, 10–12; Kafka and Iceberg/Delta documentation.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

### Quality, observability and operations

`data-engineering/05-quality-and-observability`

**What it is**

Everything else: references.md.

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** define quality dimensions (Schelter et al.); write batch tests and time-aware monitors (Beyer et al.); use lineage to bound an incident; contain and repair safely; write a blameless playbook and postmortem; run an expand-contract migration with no downtime; prove a backup by restoring it; size connections and diagnose bloat and replica lag; build slim CI with `state:modified+` and `--defer`; set a freshness SLO and page on symptoms.

**Learning:** reading order and deck. **Evidence:** catch a planted fault, trace its impact, execute an idempotent repair, and add a preventive check. **Tools:** Great Expectations, dbt, OpenLineage.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| DA 01/05 Cleaning | The assertions an analyst runs by hand |
| DA 02/01 Descriptive statistics and EDA | Looking for what is impossible |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| GA 08/11 Production and the maturity ladder | Monitoring input mix, the same idea for LLM traffic |

**Practice:** 1 deck · 96 flashcards

### ML platform

`data-engineering/06-ml-platform`

**What it is**

Everything else: references.md.

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** build point-in-time features (Kleppmann 2017, ch. 11, pp. 475–476); track experiments and models (Wilson); compare batch and online serving; detect drift (Gama et al.); select appropriate platform maturity; walk one system from framing to monitoring (Sculley et al.); read a platform's services against these ideas.

**Learning:** chapters and practice, including the DataTalks MLOps coursework. **Evidence:** a point-in-time-safe training dataset, registered run/model, served prediction, and diagnosis of drift versus pipeline failure, then a 75-minute system plan for a new problem, marked against the guide.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| DA 06/02 Credit scoring | PSI on a model's scores |
| DS 01/10 Imbalance, errors and drift | Simulated shifts on a credit model |
| DS 01/11 Choosing a metric and DS 01/12 Reading the errors | The gate's metric and its slices, for chapter 6 |
| DS 01/13 Automate, review or reject | The priced router in chapter 6 |
| DS 01/14 Learning from corrections | The audit and the correction loop in chapter 6 |
| DS 05/04 Entity matching and learning to rank | Matching bank lines to invoices in chapter 6 |
| AIE 08/02 Document understanding | The invoice reader and its checks in chapter 6 |
| GA 08/02 Defining correct | Success criteria and the two-expert test in chapter 6 |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| AIE 07/06 Serving at scale | Deployment shapes, rollout and the registry |

**Practice:** 1 deck · 77 flashcards

### Architecture and Governance

`data-engineering/07-architecture-and-governance`

**What it is**

Everything else: references.md.

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** compare centralised, mesh, fabric, and MDW architectures (Serra; Dehghani); design ownership and interoperability; control access; protect PII; account for deletion, retention, and audit obligations (Kleppmann 2017, ch. 11–12); run a catalog and a semantic layer, and trace a contract change to named consumers.

**Learning:** chapters and scenarios; the scenario prompts in data architectures are written as interview practice. **Evidence:** defend an architecture with mechanisms and trade-offs, then write an access/retention decision for a dataset, and list who a column change breaks.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| DE 02/04 Data contracts | Chapter 03 takes contract mechanics as read |
| DE 05/06 DataOps | Chapter 03 starts from its metric compiler, exposures and manifest catalog |
| DA 06/11 Metric trees | Recommended, not required: what a metric definition must state |
| Programming | Python and SQL fluency, which the zoomcamps assume |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| GA 07/05 Data-analysis agent | The agent's required metric definitions are a small semantic layer; chapter 03 is the production version |
| GA 02/08 Agentic RAG in production | Text-to-SQL schema subsetting needs the table and column meaning a catalog holds; separates PRINCE's few-shot "semantic layer" from the metric kind |

## Generative AI

LLM fundamentals, RAG, agentic foundations and frameworks, RL and alignment, observability, applications, evaluation and LLMs from scratch.

**Course outcomes**

By the end, you can explain the lifecycle and behaviour of LLMs; build and improve a retrieval system; design tool-using agents; compare frameworks; reason about RL and post-training; observe and evaluate systems; build applications; and implement key model mechanics from scratch.

**Before you begin**

No formal prerequisite is required for Unit 01. Programming: Python makes labs substantially easier; Data Analytics and Data Science deepen evaluation and modelling topics.

**Learning model:** read chapters in order, review with the deck, complete the pillar lab where supplied, and save the stated evidence. API keys, GPUs, and optional external services are never assumed unless the relevant README explicitly says so.

**Completion standard and next steps**

Complete a portfolio containing a measured RAG system, a bounded agent design or implementation, an observability/evaluation report, and one end-to-end application. Each must name its task, data boundary, success metric, failure modes, and release or stop decision.

Continue to AI Engineering for production engineering discipline, or revisit Data Science for modelling depth.

### LLM Fundamentals

`generative-ai/01-llm-fundamentals`

**What it is**

What a large language model is, how it is trained and adapted, and how to instruct, call and run it. An LLM is a decoder-only transformer trained to predict the next token. Pretraining gives it knowledge, supervised fine-tuning gives it the assistant format, and preference optimization gives it judgment. Prompts, the API and local runtimes are how you put that to work.

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** explain tokenisation, embeddings, attention (Vaswani et al.), scale, decoding, pretraining, instruction tuning, preference optimisation, PEFT, prompting, and local/open-weight inference (Alammar and Grootendorst); call models from code and diagnose hallucination.

**Learning:** chapters, deck, and practice. **Evidence:** a small model experiment with documented prompt, parameters, output behaviour, and limits.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| DS 03/01 Text representation | Optional background: the word counts that came before embeddings |
| DS 03/04 Embeddings and similarity | Optional background: word2vec and similarity |
| DS 02/01 Neural networks fundamentals | Required for chapters 09, 14 and 15, optional before them: backprop, tensors and the PyTorch training loop that those chapters' code assumes. GA 09/01 also builds backprop from scratch |
| DS 02/02 Optimisation and regularisation | Required for chapters 09, 14 and 15, optional before them: AdamW, the learning-rate sweep and the optimiser state that pretraining and the memory arithmetic in chapter 15 use |
| DS 02/05 Sequence models | Optional background: the recurrent networks attention grew out of |
| DS 02/05b Transformer encoders | Optional background: the encoder as a model you choose, with masks and position measured |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| AIE 01 Prompt engineering | Prompting starts from how the model reads tokens |
| AIE 07 Inference engineering | Serving what this pillar explains |
| AIE 08 Multimodal engineering | Extends the model beyond text |

**Practice:** 1 deck · 152 flashcards

### RAG & Retrieval

`generative-ai/02-rag-retrieval`

**What it is**

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) gives a language model external knowledge at inference time. It fetches relevant documents, puts them into the context and lets the model write the answer from them. It was the first widely deployed pattern that turned LLMs from demos into production systems.

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** design ingestion, chunking, indexing, hybrid retrieval, reranking, generation, production, agentic, cached, and managed RAG choices (Lewis et al. 2020; Huyen, ch. 6); measure the bottleneck before tuning.

**Learning:** chapters and practice. **Evidence:** a retrieval system with a query set, retrieval/generation measurements, and a justified improvement. The deck is in progress.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| DE 07/03 Catalog, semantic layer and contracts | Optional background: the table and column meaning text-to-SQL schema subsetting needs, and the metric kind of semantic layer |
| DS 03/01 Text representation | Optional background: TF-IDF, derived from scratch |
| DS 03/02 Text classification | Optional background: TF-IDF with a linear model as the baseline |
| DS 03/04 Embeddings and similarity | Optional background: BM25 built by hand |
| DS 03/07 LLM embeddings as features | Optional background: what a sentence embedding is |
| DS 01/04 Model evaluation | Optional background: the confusion matrix under recall@k |
| DS 05/02 Collaborative filtering | Optional background: recall and NDCG computed on a ranking |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| AIE 02/04 Retrieval | Retrieval as a context decision |
| AIE 03/05 Verification loops | Checking agentic RAG's output |
| AIE 05/11 KG-RAG and text-to-Cypher | Retrieval over a graph |
| AIE 08/03 Multimodal RAG | The same pipeline with images |

**Practice:** 1 deck · 195 flashcards · 24 questions · 3 worked problems

### Agentic Foundations

`generative-ai/03-agentic-foundations`

**What it is**

This pillar is where an LLM call becomes an agent: a model in a loop that chooses tools, remembers across turns and works with other agents. It covers the mechanics framework-agnostically, then shows how each framework packages them.

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** define an agent boundary (Li, ch. 1); design tool use and ReAct loops (Yao et al. 2022); externalise memory; choose multi-agent patterns; set autonomy and guardrails.

**Learning:** chapters and deck. **Evidence:** a bounded agent design specifying goals, tools, state, verifier, stop conditions, and failure handling.

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| DA 04/03b Bandits and Thompson sampling | Optional background: Thompson sampling, the simplest form of the explore-exploit idea agents inherit |
| GA 01, in this subject | The pillar starts from it |

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| AIE 02/06 Memory | Memory as a context decision |
| AIE 03/01 What a harness is | The harness around this loop |
| AIE 03/03 Tool design | Designing what the agent calls |
| AIE 04/02 Loop shapes | Stopping rules, starting from the round cap |

### Agentic Frameworks

`generative-ai/04-agentic-frameworks`

**What it is**

The hands-on framework layer: how the agent ideas from 03 and from ai-engineering's loop and graph pillars look in real code, and how to choose between the frameworks that implement them.

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** compare orchestration abstractions, state, tools, memory, tracing, and deployment trade-offs (LangGraph docs; ADK docs); avoid confusing framework choice with agent design.

**Learning:** chapters, deck, and practice. **Evidence:** the same narrow workflow implemented or specified in two frameworks with a reasoned choice.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| None in other subjects | Starts from GA 03 in this subject |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| AIE 05/02 Graph primitives | The primitives under the frameworks |
| AIE 02/10 Tools and multi-agent | Context in multi-agent systems |
| AIE 03/07 Orchestration | Orchestration from the harness side |

**Practice:** 1 deck · 88 flashcards

- **[Lab: one research agent, built several ways](../data/courses/generative-ai/04-agentic-frameworks/practice/01-research-agent/readme.md)** — The pillar's running example. One agent answers "Why do framework agents get stuck in loops, and what stops them?" over a six-document corpus, and it is built with no framework, in LangGraph and in Google ADK.

### RL & Alignment

`generative-ai/05-rl`

**What it is**

Reinforcement learning is how a pretrained model becomes an assistant. It is also, more and more, how agents learn to plan, call tools and recover from mistakes. This pillar starts in plain language, moves to the RL vocabulary (MDPs, value and policy methods, exploration) and then follows RL through the LLM stack: RLHF, preference optimization, Constitutional AI and post-training for agents.

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** reason about MDPs, value functions, policy optimisation (Sutton and Barto), reward design, RLHF, constitutional approaches, and their failure modes (Li, ch. 8).

**Learning:** chapters, deck, and practice. **Evidence:** a policy/value exercise plus a description of reward, feedback loop, and likely reward-hacking failure.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| DA 04/03b Bandits and Thompson sampling | Optional background: bandits and Thompson sampling, the case with no state |
| DA 06/08 States and sequences | Optional background: a Markov chain with rewards is an MDP without actions |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| AIE 09 Safety and security | Alignment training (RLHF, constitutional AI) sits behind its guardrails |
| AIE 04/08 Self-improving loops | Lessons that may go into post-training |
| AIE 02/06 Memory | Parametric memory |

**Practice:** 1 deck · 145 flashcards

### Observability

`generative-ai/06-observability`

**What it is**

Observability for LLM applications means you can see what happened inside a run: which tools were called, what the model received and returned, how long each step took and what it cost, and whether the output was any good. This pillar is the hands-on tooling for that, with Langfuse as the main platform and LangSmith as the alternative.

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** instrument traces (Dong et al.); create datasets; score and compare runs; diagnose failures (Beyer et al., ch. 6); manage latency and cost; turn observations into changes.

**Learning:** chapters, deck, and practice. **Evidence:** a trace review with a named failure, measurement, intervention, and regression check.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| DA 05/05 Dashboards and storytelling | Optional background: what a dashboard is for and what it can hide |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| AIE 02/09 Protocols | MCP, whose observability use is taught here |

**Practice:** 1 deck · 72 flashcards

### Agentic Applications

`generative-ai/07-agentic-applications`

**What it is**

Agentic applications are what the earlier pillars build toward: a model, tools, a loop and a deployment that complete a real task without step-by-step human direction. This pillar holds built and studied projects, not framework reference:

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** assemble retrieval, tools, memory, evaluation, and controls into scoped application patterns, including research (Du et al.; Li, ch. 6) and data-analysis agents.

**Learning:** chapters, deck, and practice. **Evidence:** a working application with enforced limits, an independent check on its answers (held-out tests or recomputed numbers), and a scoped, costed product case.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| DE 07/03 Catalog, semantic layer and contracts | Optional background: the production version of the agent's required metric definitions |
| DA 01 practice data | Required for the note 05 lab only: it reads `tips.csv` and `titanic/train.csv` from there |
| DA 01/05 Cleaning | Optional background: the checks a data-analysis agent should run |
| DA 01/07 Group-by and aggregation | Optional background: rates and group sizes |
| DA 02/04 Estimation | Optional background: whether a lead is within noise |
| DA 03/04 Correlation and causation | Optional background: A correlation written up as a cause |
| Data analytics | Optional background: A data-analysis agent does the analyst's job, and the rules of thumb are the checks it should run |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| AIE 03/04 Execution boundaries | Where these agents may act |
| AIE 03/07 Orchestration | Coordinating them |
| AIE 05/04 Durable execution and interrupts | Pausing and resuming them |
| AIE 08/04 Speech and voice agents | Agents with a clock on them |

### Eval Engineering

`generative-ai/08-eval`

**What it is**

Eval engineering is the discipline of measuring non-deterministic AI systems: defining what "correct" means, building tasks that represent real use, choosing graders you can trust, running them repeatedly, reading the results with error bars, and wiring them into CI, releases and production monitoring.

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** design task datasets (Anthropic, *Demystifying evals*); choose metrics; use human and model judges cautiously (Husain and Shankar); analyse uncertainty; test regressions; make evaluation a release gate; evaluate agents by outcome, trajectory, components and repeated trials; evaluate RAG retrieval and grounding separately; trace runs so scores attach to spans; sample and review production traffic; calibrate a judge against human labels before it gates anything.

**Learning:** chapters, deck, and practice. **Evidence:** a versioned evaluation report with failure taxonomy, confidence/uncertainty, and a CI or release decision.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| DA 02/02 Probability foundations | Optional background: base rates under a judge's pass rate |
| DA 02/04 Estimation | Optional background: standard errors, Wilson intervals and the bootstrap |
| DA 02/05 Hypothesis testing | Optional background: paired tests and multiple comparisons |
| DA 03/01 A/B testing | Optional background: sample size and peeking |
| DA 03/03 Experimentation at scale | Optional background: SRM, CUPED and looking early |
| DS 01/04 Model evaluation | Optional background: the confusion matrix under judges and retrieval metrics |
| DS 02/03 Structuring ML projects | Optional background: error analysis and dev and test sets |
| DS 01/12 Reading the errors | Optional background: the procedure behind 02's error analysis and 14's reading of a judge |
| GA 06/03 Judge pipelines over trace exports | The planted failures and rule judges behind note 14's exercise |
| DS 05/02 Collaborative filtering | Optional background: recall and NDCG |
