### LLMs from scratch

`generative-ai/09-llms-from-scratch`

**What it is**

Three chapters built on Karpathy's *Neural Networks: Zero to Hero*: micrograd (a backpropagation engine in about 100 lines), makemore (character-level language models, from counted bigrams to a WaveNet-style tree) and nanoGPT (a transformer on Tiny Shakespeare, then GPT-2 124M with OpenAI's weights loaded in). Watch the lectures and code along in the notebooks. The chapters are what you come back to, with every CPU-sized experiment rerun and its output shown.

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** implement automatic differentiation, train a character model, and understand the path from micrograd to a compact GPT (Karpathy; Raschka).

**Learning:** chapters, deck, and practice. **Evidence:** a modified training run with hypothesis, metric, result, and explanation.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| DS 01/02 Supervised learning | Optional background: logistic regression, the first rung |
| DS 02/01 Neural networks fundamentals | Optional background: backprop and the training loop |
| DS 02/05b Transformer encoders | Optional background: the encoder side, which nanoGPT omits |
| DS 02/06 Neural text models | Optional background: LSTM and BERT pipelines, compared |
| DS 03/02 Text classification | Optional background: TF-IDF with a linear model, compared |
| DS 03/04 Embeddings and similarity | Optional background: word2vec, compared |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| AIE 07 Inference engineering | Serving the model you built |

**Practice:** 1 deck · 57 flashcards

## AI Engineering

The engineering disciplines on top of models: prompt, context, harness, loop and graph, plus inference, multimodal, safety and security.

**Course outcomes**

By the end, you can design prompts and context deliberately; build verifiable harnesses; create bounded autonomous loops and multi-agent graphs; reason about inference cost and performance; build multimodal features; and threat-model, test, and respond to unsafe systems.

**Before you begin**

**Required:** Generative AI Unit 01 and practical Python or TypeScript. Generative AI Unit 08 is a strong companion for measurement. Data Analytics helps with experiments and metrics.

**Learning model:** take Units 01 to 05 in order. Unit 07 is the serving substrate; Units 08 and 09 are cross-cuts to use whenever the system has non-text inputs or meaningful risk. Study notes, review decks, complete side-by-side Python/TypeScript labs when present, and retain mastery evidence.

**Completion standard and next steps**

Complete a portfolio containing a measured prompt/context design, tested harness, bounded loop or graph, inference trade-off analysis, and a threat model with red-team evidence. Each item must identify the task, operator control, verification method, cost or latency budget, failure mode, and stop/rollback condition.

For model fundamentals and application patterns, return to Generative AI; for data and evaluation foundations, use Data Analytics and Data Science.

### Prompt Engineering

`ai-engineering/01-prompt-engineering`

**What it is**

Prompt engineering is the craft of writing instructions and examples that consistently elicit desired outputs from language models. It governs what you *ask* and how you *structure* the ask — not what you put in the context window alongside it. Once you're deciding *which documents, memory or tool outputs to include*, you're doing context engineering (02-context).

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** turn a task into instructions, examples, structure, and constraints (Berryman and Ziegler); diagnose prompt failures from each note's Pitfalls and the tool-call debugging table; compare prompt variants with an evaluation set (Huyen, ch. 5); account for adversarial prompting.

**Learning:** chapters, deck, and practice. **Evidence:** a versioned prompt with task cases, pass criteria, and observed failure modes.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| DA 03/01 A/B testing | Optional background: prompt A against prompt B online |
| GA 01 LLM fundamentals | How models read text, which prompting assumes |
| GA 08/09 Reading results | Whether a prompt change is real |
| GA 08/04 Graders | How prompt outputs get scored |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| Context engineering | Prompts are one layer of the context it composes |
| Safety and security | Builds on the attack catalogue and prompt-level defences in 10 |
| GA 01/10 Prompting fundamentals | The application-level summary of this pillar, written for readers of GA 01 |

### Context Engineering

`ai-engineering/02-context-engineering`

**What it is**

Context engineering is the discipline of composing what goes *into* the model's context window on each call: which documents to include, which memory to surface, which tool outputs to inject, how much history to keep, and how to spend the token budget across all of it. Prompt engineering ends at the instructions themselves (01-prompt-engineering). Context engineering begins with the surrounding content those instructions act on.

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** model a context window as a budget (Mei et al.); select, compress, retrieve, and order information; preserve state across sessions; test context quality rather than assuming more is better (Chroma, *Context Rot*).

**Learning:** chapters, deck, and practice. **Evidence:** a context budgeter or design showing inclusion rules, truncation policy, measurements, and failure cases.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| GA 02/01 RAG pipeline | Retrieval mechanics |
| GA 03/03 Memory as an operating system | Memory mechanics |
| GA 08/07 Evaluating RAG and grounding | Retrieval metrics |
| DS 01/04 Model evaluation | Optional background: recall and precision as cells of the table |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| None in other subjects | AI engineering is the last subject in the path |

### Harness Engineering

`ai-engineering/03-harness-engineering`

**What it is**

Harness engineering is the scaffolding discipline: building the infrastructure that makes agent loops deployable, reliable, observable, and safe. A harness wraps one or more agent loops and provides tool execution, memory integration, guardrails, permissions, observability hooks, error handling, and verification. It is the layer at which eval/memory/observability become **first-class primitives** (not afterthoughts).

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** build a system around a model with tool boundaries, state, verification, retries, scope, and an auditable definition of done (Anthropic, *Effective harnesses*; OpenAI, *Harness engineering*).

**Learning:** chapters, deck, and practice. **Evidence:** a harness with a task contract, independent checks, recorded state, and a safe stop path.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| GA 03/01 Agent fundamentals | The loop a harness wraps |
| GA 02/08 Agentic RAG: PRINCE | The worked example |
| GA 08/06 Evaluating agents | Measuring a harness |
| GA 08/10 Evals in CI | Checks as gates |
| DS 01/04 Model evaluation | Optional background: false positives and their cost |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| None in other subjects | AI engineering is the last subject in the path |

**Practice:** 1 deck · 117 flashcards

### Loop Engineering

`ai-engineering/04-loop-engineering`

**What it is**

Loop engineering is the discipline of designing the system that prompts your agent, so you don't have to. A loop is **a goal plus a scorekeeper, repeated, with a way to stop**: each cycle gathers context, acts, verifies, and either saves the change or rolls it back.

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** design goal-plus-scorekeeper cycles; select stop conditions and autonomy (Albada); externalise state (Oshin and Campos); handle long-running execution; identify loop failure modes.

**Learning:** chapters, deck, and paired labs. **Evidence:** a Python or TypeScript loop that runs offline, records iterations, uses an independent scorekeeper, and ends with an explicit reason.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| GA 03/01 Agent fundamentals | The round cap and exits |
| GA 08/04 Graders | The scorekeeper's scoring |
| GA 08/12 Failure modes | Goodhart: the eval becomes the target |
| DA 04/03b Bandits and Thompson sampling | Optional background: Bayesian bandits for live traffic |
| GA 05 RL | Post-training as the end of the lesson path |

**Feeds into**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| None in other subjects | AI engineering is the last subject in the path |

**Practice:** 1 deck · 98 flashcards

### Graph Engineering

`ai-engineering/05-graph-engineering`

**What it is**

Graph engineering is the discipline of designing the **topology** of a multi-agent system: which nodes exist (agents, deterministic functions, routers, human checkpoints), which transitions between them are permitted, and how the runtime work graph forms and mutates. Where loop engineering governs individual agent behavior (one agent, one cycle), graph engineering governs the structure connecting multiple agents — conditional routing, shared state, parallel fan-out and merge, and hierarchical composition of subgraphs.

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** choose a graph only when structure is earned (Anthropic, *Building effective agents*); design routing, fan-out, reducers, retries, approvals, checkpoints, and knowledge-graph integration (Negro et al.); distinguish agent graphs from knowledge graphs.

**Learning:** chapters, deck, and paired labs. **Evidence:** a graph design or implementation with state schema, transition rules, failure handling, and a justification for every added node.

**Builds on and feeds into**

| GA 04/01 ReAct from scratch to LangGraph and GA 04/02 Agentic search, persistence and streaming | LangGraph mechanics (nodes, edges, state, checkpoints) taught first there; notes 02-04 here assume them |
| GA 03/01 Agent fundamentals and GA 03/04 Multi-agent design patterns | Multi-agent vocabulary for notes 05-07 |
| GA 02/01 The RAG pipeline | Vector RAG, which note 11 extends with graphs |
| DS 01 Classical ML and DS 02/01 Neural networks fundamentals | Only for notes 13 and 13b (features, classifiers, embeddings) |
| DS 03/05 Semantic analysis and NER | Optional background: NER without an LLM |
| GA 08/03 Eval datasets | Building the gold set for extraction |
| GA 08/07 Evaluating RAG and grounding | Scoring KG-RAG |

### Inference Engineering

`ai-engineering/07-inference-engineering`

**What it is**

Inference engineering is the discipline of making a model answer fast enough and cheaply enough to ship: how requests are batched and scheduled, where the KV cache lives, how prefill and decode are split across hardware, and what the GPU is actually doing while it waits. The layers above it decide *what* the model is asked. This layer decides what each of those calls costs in latency, throughput and money.

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** relate latency, throughput, and cost to hardware, batching, KV cache, prefill/decode, kernels, compilers, serving, and adaptive inference (Fregly; Huyen, ch. 9).

**Learning:** chapters and practice. **Evidence:** a profiling or serving proposal that names the bottleneck, expected trade-off, metric, and rollback criterion.

**Builds on and feeds into**

|---|---|
| DS 02/01 Neural networks fundamentals | Optional background: what a forward pass is |
| GA 01 LLM fundamentals | How transformers work |
| DE 06/02 Experiment tracking and model registry | Optional background: versions, labels and rollback |
| DE 06/03 Batch and online serving | Optional background: serving shapes for an ordinary ML model |

|---|---|
| None in other subjects | AI engineering is the last subject in the path |

### Multimodal Engineering

`ai-engineering/08-multimodal-engineering`

**What it is**

Multimodal engineering is building systems whose inputs or outputs include images, documents, audio or video: sending a screenshot or a scanned invoice to a vision model, retrieving over pages that are mostly figures and tables, transcribing a call, speaking a reply, generating an image. Each modality changes the six layers above it. An image costs tokens the way text does, but you can't skim it to trim the budget. A PDF page can be retrieved as pixels instead of extracted text. A voice agent has a latency budget measured in hundreds of milliseconds. A vision answer can't be graded by string match.

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** design image/document input, multimodal retrieval, voice, image/video generation (Phoenix and Taylor, ch. 7 and 9), and multimodal evaluation with explicit quality, latency, and provenance constraints.

**Learning:** chapters and references. **Evidence:** a modality feature specification or prototype with an input-quality check, budget, evaluation set, and fallback.

**Builds on and feeds into**

**Builds on**

| Chapter | Why |
|---|---|
| DS 02/01 Neural networks fundamentals and DS 02/02 Optimisation and regularisation | Required for chapter 02b: the PyTorch training loop, `DataLoader` and optimiser that 02b scales up to a vision-language model |
| DS 02/04 Convolutional networks | Optional background: the vision models before ViT |
| DS 02/04b Vision in practice | Recommended before chapter 02b: an image `Dataset`, torchvision transforms and the weights' preprocessing preset, and freezing a pretrained backbone with BatchNorm kept in eval mode |
| DS 03/04 Embeddings and similarity | Optional background: comparing vectors in one space |
| GA 01/09 PEFT and fine-tuning in practice | Parameter-efficient fine-tuning on text models, the method chapter 02b applies to a vision-language model |
| GA 02/01 RAG pipeline | The pipeline multimodal RAG extends |
| GA 07/03 Real-time multimodal agents | The voice case |
| GA 08/05 LLM as judge | Judging multimodal output |
| GA 08/15 Calibrating a judge | One calibration set per modality |

### Safety and Security

`ai-engineering/09-safety-and-security`

**What it is**

Safety and security engineering is keeping an LLM system from being turned against its users or its owner, and from harming either by accident. The security half has an adversary: prompt injection, data exfiltration through tool calls, poisoned retrieval, leaked system prompts, supply-chain attacks on models and MCP servers. The safety half has none: harmful or biased output, private data surfacing in an answer, an agent taking an irreversible action it was never meant to take. Both come from the same root. The model can't reliably tell instructions from data, so a system that mixes untrusted input, private data and the ability to act outside itself (Willison's "lethal trifecta") will eventually be exploited.

**From the syllabus**

**Objectives:** threat-model an LLM system (OWASP Top 10 for LLM); resist prompt injection and unsafe tool use (Debenedetti et al.); select guardrails and sandboxes; red-team systems; protect data and supply chain; prepare an incident response.

**Learning:** chapters and references. **Evidence:** a threat model, one red-team test, layered mitigation plan, and incident response path.

**Builds on and feeds into**

| GA 05/04 Constitutional AI | Alignment training |
